 h^0(F^*P_1\otimes L)=\dim\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}(G_{p,L},P_1).$$ Indeed $F^!P_1=F^*P_1\otimes K_X^{1-p}$, so adjunction applied to $K_X^{1-p}\otimes L^{-1}$ proves the identity. The semilinearity of absolute Frobenius does not change these dimensions. Apply <a href="#eq:frobenius-presentation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frobenius-presentation">[eq:frobenius-presentation]</a>. The obstruction to injectivity of $$\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}(G_{p,L},P_1)\longrightarrow
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}(\mathcal O_X(-j)^{N_{p,L}},P_1)$$ is the image of $$\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}(K_{p,L}[1],P_1)
 =\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}(K_{p,L},\mathcal H^{-1}(P_1)).$$ The equality uses the ordinary cohomological range of $P_1$. Generation of $K_{p,L}(j_2)$ and the assumed vanishing make this group zero. The Hom map is therefore injective, and its target has dimension $N_{p,L}h^0(P_1(j))\leq C_0p^3h^0(P_1(j))$. ◻

</details>

Thus the characteristic-zero estimates we shall need are a section bound at $j$ and a vanishing for the negative cohomology sheaf at $j_2$. They will pass to the chosen fibers by semicontinuity. We now choose the remaining constants so that both estimates can be proved uniformly in the tilt object.

<a id="eq:apex-m-choice"></a>
<a id="eq:apex-A-choice"></a>
Fix $m$ and the compact smooth neighborhoods of surfaces and curves from Lemma <a href="#lem:uniform-sections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:uniform-sections">23</a>. In particular, $$\label{eq:apex-m-choice}
 h^0(\mathcal O_S(m))=\chi(\mathcal O_S(m)),\qquad
 \mu(K_S)-m\leq-1.$$ Put $M=\max(j,j_2)$, choose $c>M+2$, and then choose $A$ so large that Proposition <a href="#prop:negative-surface-cohomology" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:negative-surface-cohomology">27</a> applies for upper twist $M$ and both $B_0,-B_0$, and $$\label{eq:apex-A-choice}
 A>c,\qquad A>j_2+2,\qquad A\geq1,\qquad
 A^2-c^2\geq\delta_{\rm line}:=-HB_0^2/h.$$ The number $\delta_{\rm line}$ is nonnegative by the Hodge index theorem. We shall choose one further integer $m_0$ below, depending only on these constants, and put $t_0=-m_0$. Thus $A$ does not depend on the negative endpoint $t_0$: the negative-surface estimate requires an upper bound on the twists for its positivity, and its constant may subsequently depend on the chosen finite range.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### The two decompositions at slope zero

Fix a finite-slope semistable $E$ at $(A,b)$ with $|b|\leq1$ and $\nu_{A,b}(E)=0$. Suppress $B=B_0+bH$ in numerical subscripts when there is no ambiguity. Write $$V=\mathcal H^{-1}(E),\qquad U=\mathcal H^0(E),\qquad
 \bar U=U/U_{\rm tor}.$$ The sheaf $V$ is torsion-free. All the ordinary slope factors of $V$ have slope at most $b-A$, and all those of $\bar U$ have slope at least $b+A$. To prove the first assertion, the highest slope factor $V_1[1]$ is a subobject of $E$ in the first-tilt heart. Finite-slope semistability excludes a denominator-zero subobject and gives $$w_B(V_1)\geq A^2r(V_1)/2.$$ Classical Bogomolov–Gieseker gives $2w_B(V_1)/r(V_1)\leq(\mu(V_1)-b)^2$; the first torsion pair gives $\mu(V_1)\leq b$. Together they give $\mu(V_1)\leq b-A$. For the other assertion use the lowest slope factor of $\bar U$, which is a quotient of $E$, and the same computation with $\mu>b$.

Throughout this section, $O(e)$ denotes an absolute value bounded by a fixed constant times $e=d-A|r|$. Constants in this notation may depend on the choices above, but not on $E$, its rank, or $b$. Rank and character bounds for a complex do not mean bounds for the length of its zero-dimensional cohomology.

For nonnegative rank, we retain a quotient with slopes within one unit of $b+A$ and bound sections of the residual complex by $e$. For nonpositive rank, we retain a shifted subsheaf with slopes within one unit of $b-A$; its surface first cohomology will allow us to bound all sections of $E$ by $e$. This second bound will also be applied to the shifted dual of a nonnegative-rank object, controlling its degree-two cohomology.

<a id="eq:positive-excess"></a>
<a id="eq:positive-residual-triangle"></a>
Suppose first that $r(E)\geq0$. On refined ordinary slope filtrations the equality $e=d-Ar$ reads $$\label{eq:positive-excess}
 e=\sum_{\bar U}r_i(\mu_i-b-A)
   +d(U_{\rm tor})+\sum_Vr_i(b-\mu_i+A).$$ Every term is nonnegative. Let $Q$ be the slope tail quotient of $\bar U$ whose slopes lie in $[b+A,b+A+1]$, and let $P$ be the kernel of the epimorphism $E\to Q$ in the tilt heart: $$\label{eq:positive-residual-triangle}
 P\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow Q.$$ Empty parts mean zero sheaves. The negative cohomology of $P$ is $V$, and its degree-zero cohomology is an extension of the slope prefix with slopes $>b+A+1$ by $U_{\rm tor}$. The corresponding sheaf sequences, shifted where appropriate, are exact in the tilt heart because their torsion-free terms stay in the indicated halves of the torsion pair.

<a id="eq:residual-character-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:positive-tail-moments"></a>
Equation <a href="#eq:positive-excess" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-excess">[eq:positive-excess]</a> gives $$\label{eq:residual-character-bound}
 d(P),\ |r(P)|,\ r(\mathcal H^{-1}(P)),\
 r(\mathcal H^0(P))=O(e).$$ For example, on the high prefix the positive number $\mu_i-b-A>1$ controls both $r_i$ and $r_i(\mu_i-b)$; on $V$ the number $b-\mu_i+A\geq2A$ does the same. The torsion degree is at most $e$. Quotient comparison and the classical discriminant inequality on the factors of $Q$ give $$\label{eq:positive-tail-moments}
 \frac{A^2r(Q)}2\leq w_B(Q)
 \leq\frac{A^2r(Q)}2+O(e),\qquad
 \sum_Qr_i\delta_i^B=O(e).$$ Indeed, write $\mu_i-b=A+s_i$, where $0\leq s_i\leq1$ and $\sum r_is_i\leq e$. Then $\sum r_i(\mu_i-b)^2=A^2r(Q)+O(e)$, and subtract $2w_B(Q)$. Additivity with $w_B(E)=A^2r(E)/2$ now also gives $w_B(P)=O(e)$.

<a id="eq:negative-residual-triangle"></a>
<a id="eq:negative-tail-h1"></a>
For $r(E)\leq0$ use instead <a id="eq:negative-excess"></a>$$\label{eq:negative-excess}
 e=\sum_Vr_i(b-A-\mu_i)
   +\sum_{\bar U}r_i(\mu_i-b+A)+d(U_{\rm tor}).$$ Let $V_0$ be the slope prefix of $V$ with slopes in $[b-A-1,b-A]$ and form the exact triangle in the tilt heart $$\label{eq:negative-residual-triangle}
 V_0[1]\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow P.$$ The same estimates give <a href="#eq:residual-character-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:residual-character-bound">[eq:residual-character-bound]</a> and $w_B(P)=O(e)$. More explicitly, subobject comparison applied to $V_0[1]$ gives $$A^2r(V_0)/2\leq w_B(V_0)\leq A^2r(V_0)/2+O(e).$$ Put $T=B-AH$. If $\mu_i-b=-A-s_i$ with $0\leq s_i\leq1$, then $\sum r_is_i\leq e$ and $$\sum_{V_0}r_i\mu_T^2\leq e,\qquad
 w_T(V_0)=w_B(V_0)+A d_B(V_0)+A^2r(V_0)/2\geq-Ae.$$ Since $\delta_i^T=\mu_T^2-2w_T(G_i)/r_i$, it follows that <a id="eq:negative-tail-energy"></a>$$\label{eq:negative-tail-energy}
 \sum_{V_0}r_i(\mu_T^2+\delta_i^T)=O(e).$$ After very general restriction to a surface in the fixed smooth neighborhood and refinement into stable factors, this bound remains valid by Lemma <a href="#lem:restriction-variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:restriction-variance">22</a>. For each such factor, $$\mu_T^2+\delta^T=\|c_0/r-T\|^2+\xi.$$ Consequently Proposition <a href="#prop:negative-surface-cohomology" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:negative-surface-cohomology">27</a> implies, for every integer $t_0<t\leq M$, $$\label{eq:negative-tail-h1}
 h^1\bigl(V_0|_S(t)\bigr)=O(e).$$ Both decompositions are available when $r(E)=0$; we use the one specified by the argument in which it occurs.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Line comparisons and images of sections

We next control the sections of $P$ in <a href="#eq:positive-residual-triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-residual-triangle">[eq:positive-residual-triangle]</a>, and those of $E$ in <a href="#eq:negative-residual-triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:negative-residual-triangle">[eq:negative-residual-triangle]</a>. Their restrictions can have more sections than are bounded by $e$. The argument will bound the image of a space of sections under restriction.

<a id="eq:line-quotient-numerator"></a>
<a id="eq:line-whole-slope"></a>
We need a comparison valid for all tilt quotients of a line bundle. At parameters $(a,b')$, let $\ell\in\mathbb Z$, $L=\mathcal O_X(\ell H)$ and $u=\ell-b'>0$. A nonzero proper tilt quotient $I$ fits into $0\to K\to L\to I\to0$ in the heart, with $K$ a sheaf and $$0\longrightarrow\mathcal H^{-1}(I)\longrightarrow K
 \longrightarrow L\longrightarrow\mathcal H^0(I)\longrightarrow0.$$ The image in $L$ is nonzero: otherwise $K=\mathcal H^{-1}(I)$ belongs to both halves of the slope torsion pair and is zero, so the quotient is the whole line. The image therefore has rank one. It follows that $K$ is torsion-free, all its slopes lie in $(b',\ell]$, and $r(I)=-r(\mathcal H^{-1}(I))$. Classical Bogomolov–Gieseker on the slope factors gives $w_{B_0+b'H}(K)\leq u d(K)/2$. Since $r(L)=1$, $d(L)=u$ and $2w(L)=u^2-\delta_{\rm line}$, subtraction gives $$\label{eq:line-quotient-numerator}
 n(I)\geq\frac{u d(I)-\delta_{\rm line}
                    +a^2r(\mathcal H^{-1}(I))}{2}.$$ If $a^2\geq\delta_{\rm line}$, every finite quotient slope is at least $u/2$ when $r(I)<0$, and at least $u/2-h\delta_{\rm line}/2$ when $r(I)=0$. For the latter assertion, $d(I)>0$ lies in $h^{-1}\mathbb Z$, so $d(I)\geq1/h$. The whole line has slope $$\label{eq:line-whole-slope}
 \nu_{a,b'}(L)=\frac u2-\frac{a^2+\delta_{\rm line}}{2u}.$$

<a id="eq:initial-section-vanishing"></a>
Choose the positive integer $m_0$ so large that $$m_0-1>h\delta_{\rm line},\qquad
 (m_0-1)^2>A^2+\delta_{\rm line}.$$ At $(A,b)$ all finite quotient slopes of $\mathcal O_X(m_0H)$ are then strictly positive. A nonzero map from this line to $E$ would have an image that is both such a quotient and a subobject of the slope-zero semistable $E$. A denominator-zero image is also excluded by finite-slope semistability. Thus, with $t_0=-m_0$, $$\label{eq:initial-section-vanishing}
 h^0(E(t_0))=0,\qquad
 h^0(P(t_0))=0\quad\hbox{in the positive-rank decomposition}.$$

For the remaining estimates work at the side point $$b_s=b-c,\qquad a_s=\sqrt{A^2-c^2},\qquad B_s=B_0+b_sH.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:tilt-semicircle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tilt-semicircle">20</a> shows that $E$ is semistable there with slope $c$. Both triangles above remain short exact sequences in its tilt heart: the negative sheaf slopes are at most $b-A<b-c$, and the positive ones at least $b+A>b-c$. The numerical quantities $d(P),r(P),w(P)$ are still $O(e)$ after this bounded twist change. For any integer $t_0\leq t\leq M$, the line $\mathcal O_X(-tH)$ has $u=-t-b_s>1$. Equations <a href="#eq:line-quotient-numerator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:line-quotient-numerator">[eq:line-quotient-numerator]</a> and <a href="#eq:line-whole-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:line-whole-slope">[eq:line-whole-slope]</a> provide a single lower bound $-C_1$ for all its finite tilt quotient slopes, uniformly in this finite range.

<a id="stmt-lem-restriction-image"></a>
<div id="lem:restriction-image" class="lemma">

**Lemma 31**. *Let $E\in\mathcal B_{H,B_0+bH}$ be finite-slope $\nu_{A,b}$-semistable with $\nu_{A,b}(E)=0$ and $|b|\leq1$. Put $B=B_0+bH$ and $e=d_B(E)-A|r(E)|$. Let $P$ be the residual term in <a href="#eq:positive-residual-triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-residual-triangle">[eq:positive-residual-triangle]</a> when $r(E)\geq0$, or in <a href="#eq:negative-residual-triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:negative-residual-triangle">[eq:negative-residual-triangle]</a> when $r(E)\leq0$. Let $W\subset\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}(\mathcal O_X(-tH),P)$ be any finite-dimensional subspace, where $t_0<t\leq M$ is an integer. There is a very general smooth $S\in|H|$ in the fixed neighborhood, depending on this finite data, such that the image of $$W\longrightarrow\mathbb H^0\bigl(S,P|_S(t)\bigr)$$ has dimension at most $C_2e$. The constant is independent of $W$ and its dimension.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-restriction-image"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-restriction-image</summary>

<a id="eq:section-evaluation-sheaves"></a>
*Proof.* Put $N=\dim W$ and take the tilt image $I$ and kernel $K'$ of the evaluation map $\mathcal O_X(-tH)^N\to P$ at the side point. We first control the rank, first character, and $H$-degree of the second character of $I$ by $e$, independently of $N$. We then use the evaluation kernel, whose rank is close to $N$, to cancel all but $O(e)$ of the sections in the evaluation source. Ordinary cohomology gives a sheaf $K'$ and an exact sequence $$\label{eq:section-evaluation-sheaves}
 0\longrightarrow V_I\longrightarrow K'
 \longrightarrow\mathcal O_X(-tH)^N\longrightarrow U_I\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad V_I\subset V_P,$$ where $V_I=\mathcal H^{-1}(I)$, $U_I=\mathcal H^0(I)$ and $V_P=\mathcal H^{-1}(P)$. Since $I\subset P$, $0\leq d(I)\leq d(P)=O(e)$. The sheaf $U_I(t)$ is globally generated. Its first Chern class has nonnegative $H$-degree, including its divisorial torsion, and $H^2B_0=0$; hence $$d(U_I)\geq u r(U_I),\qquad d(V_I)\leq0.$$ Thus $r(U_I)=O(e)$. The inclusion into $V_P$ gives $r(V_I)=O(e)$ as well.

In the positive decomposition $I\subset P\subset E$, so $n(I)\leq c d(I)$. In the negative decomposition $P/I$ is a quotient of $E$ and satisfies $n(P/I)\geq c d(P/I)$, also when its denominator is zero. Additivity and the numerical bounds on $P$ therefore give $n(I)\leq O(e)$ in both cases.

<a id="eq:evaluation-image-numerics"></a>
Every finite Harder–Narasimhan slope of $I$ is at least $-C_1$. Indeed, its last factor receives a nonzero map from one of the lines in the evaluation map. The tilt image of this map is a quotient of that line and a subobject of the last semistable factor; the line comparison forces the claimed lower bound. If the last factor has infinite slope there are no finite factors. Denominator-zero factors have nonnegative numerator. Writing $d_i,n_i$ for the factors, we obtain $$\sum_i|n_i|\leq n(I)+2C_1d(I)=O(e).$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:tilt-support-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tilt-support-bound">18</a>, applied at the fixed side volume and summed over the filtration, now gives $$\|c_{B_s}(I)\|+|w_{B_s}(I)|=O(e).$$ The twist change to $I(t)$ is bounded. Its rank is $O(e)$ by <a href="#eq:section-evaluation-sheaves" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:section-evaluation-sheaves">[eq:section-evaluation-sheaves]</a>, so $$\label{eq:evaluation-image-numerics}
 \|c_0(I(t))\|+|w_0(I(t))|=O(e).$$

<a id="eq:evaluation-kernel-moments"></a>
The sheaf $K'(t)$ is torsion-free and has rank $N+O(e)$. Before twisting it belongs to the positive part of the torsion pair, while <a href="#eq:section-evaluation-sheaves" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:section-evaluation-sheaves">[eq:section-evaluation-sheaves]</a> bounds its maximum slope by $-t$. Its slopes after twisting therefore lie in $(-u,0]$. Its first and second characters are the negatives of those in <a href="#eq:evaluation-image-numerics" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:evaluation-image-numerics">[eq:evaluation-image-numerics]</a>. For its refined ordinary slope factors this proves $$\label{eq:evaluation-kernel-moments}
 \sum_i r_i\mu_i^2+\sum_i r_i\delta_i^0=O(e).$$ In detail, the bounded nonpositive slope interval and the total degree $O(e)$ bound the first sum; subtracting the total $2w_0$ bounds the second.

The next step is to obtain almost as many sections of this kernel on a surface as the trivial evaluation source has. Its small first character and projected second character control the Riemann–Roch error; the same numerical bounds will control the cohomology lost from the negative term $V_I$.

<a id="eq:evaluation-negative-sections"></a>
Choose a very general $S$ in the fixed neighborhood and a very general curve $C_m\in|mH|_S$ in its fixed neighborhood. Impose also the finitely many open conditions that all the sheaf sequences and filtrations used here restrict exactly, with torsion-free restrictions where required. Such choices are possible by Lemma <a href="#lem:uniform-sections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:uniform-sections">23</a>. Intersect the refined restriction filtrations of $K'(t)$ with $V_I(t)$. The successive intersections are subsheaves of the stable restriction factors and have total rank $O(e)$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:restriction-variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:restriction-variance">22</a>, the second slope moments on both $S$ and $C_m$ are $O(e)$. Applying Lemma <a href="#lem:subsheaf-sections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:subsheaf-sections">25</a> to the intersections, after the additional fixed twist $m$, yields $$\label{eq:evaluation-negative-sections}
 h^0\bigl(V_I|_S(t+m)\bigr)+
 h^0\bigl(V_I|_{C_m}(t+m)\bigr)=O(e).$$ The reason this has no $N$ term is that each bound is a constant times the rank of the intersection plus its rank times $\mu_{i,+}^2$; its rank is at most $r_i$, and the second moments in <a href="#eq:evaluation-kernel-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:evaluation-kernel-moments">[eq:evaluation-kernel-moments]</a> control the sum.

<a id="eq:kernel-many-sections"></a>
We also have $h^2(K'|_S(t+m))=O(e)$. A stable factor of $K'(t)|_S$ with slope $\mu_i>\mu(K_S)-m$ has no homomorphism to $K_S(-m)$. For all the other factors, <a href="#eq:apex-m-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:apex-m-choice">[eq:apex-m-choice]</a> gives $\mu_i\leq-1$, so their total rank, as well as their second slope moment, is $O(e)$. Serre duality and Lemma <a href="#lem:subsheaf-sections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:subsheaf-sections">25</a> applied to their dual hulls, twisted by $K_S(-m)$, bound their $h^2$ by $O(e)$. Surface Riemann–Roch and <a href="#eq:evaluation-image-numerics" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:evaluation-image-numerics">[eq:evaluation-image-numerics]</a> give $$\chi\bigl(K'|_S(t+m)\bigr)
 =r(K')\chi(\mathcal O_S(m))+O(e).$$ Indeed the error consists of $w_0(K'(t))$ and the intersection of $c_0(K'(t))$ with the fixed class $mH-K_S/2$; here $K_S=(K_X+H)|_S$. Consequently $$\label{eq:kernel-many-sections}
 h^0\bigl(K'|_S(t+m)\bigr)
 \geq r(K')h^0(\mathcal O_S(m))-O(e).$$

Restrict the triangle $K'\to\mathcal O_X(-tH)^N\to I$ and twist by $(t+m)H$. The kernel on $H^0$ from $K'|_S(t+m)$ has dimension at most $h^{-1}(I|_S(t+m))=h^0(V_I|_S(t+m))$. Equations <a href="#eq:evaluation-negative-sections" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:evaluation-negative-sections">[eq:evaluation-negative-sections]</a> and <a href="#eq:kernel-many-sections" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-many-sections">[eq:kernel-many-sections]</a>, with $r(K')=N+O(e)$, therefore give $$\begin{aligned}
 &\dim\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\!\left(
 H^0(K'|_S(t+m))\longrightarrow H^0(\mathcal O_S(m))^N
 \right)\\
 &\hspace{25mm}\geq N h^0(\mathcal O_S(m))-O(e).
 \end{aligned}$$ The source of the next map has dimension $Nh^0(\mathcal O_S(m))$. Subtracting the displayed lower bound shows that the image of $$H^0(\mathcal O_S(m))^N\longrightarrow\mathbb H^0(I|_S(t+m))$$ has dimension $O(e)$. This is the cancellation which removes the unrestricted dimension $N$ from the estimate.

It remains to remove the auxiliary twist $m$. Multiplication by the section defining $C_m$ gives a map from $\mathbb H^0(I|_S(t))$ to this latter group. Its kernel has dimension at most $h^{-1}(I|_{C_m}(t+m))$, which is again $O(e)$ by <a href="#eq:evaluation-negative-sections" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:evaluation-negative-sections">[eq:evaluation-negative-sections]</a>. The image of $H^0(\mathcal O_S)^N$ in $\mathbb H^0(I|_S(t))$ consequently has dimension $O(e)$. Composing with $I\to P$ proves the assertion for $W$. All equalities involving $h^{-1}$ use derived restriction with nonzerodivisors on the ordinary cohomology sheaves; no exactness of a tilt-image operation under restriction was assumed. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cor-residual-sections"></a>
<div id="cor:residual-sections" class="corollary">

<a id="eq:residual-sections"></a>
<a id="eq:negative-cohomology-vanishing"></a>
**Corollary 32**. *Let $E\in\mathcal B_{H,B_0+bH}$ be finite-slope $\nu_{A,b}$-semistable with $\nu_{A,b}(E)=0$ and $|b|\leq1$. Put $B=B_0+bH$ and $e=d_B(E)-A|r(E)|$. For $r(E)\geq0$, take $P$ from <a href="#eq:positive-residual-triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-residual-triangle">[eq:positive-residual-triangle]</a> and put $V_P=\mathcal H^{-1}(P)$. For $r(E)\leq0$, use the decomposition <a href="#eq:negative-residual-triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:negative-residual-triangle">[eq:negative-residual-triangle]</a> and put $V=\mathcal H^{-1}(E)$. There is a constant $C_3$, independent of $E,b$, such that $$\label{eq:residual-sections}
 \begin{aligned}
 h^0(P(j))&\leq C_3e &&\text{for the positive decomposition},\\
 h^0(E(j))&\leq C_3e &&\text{for the negative decomposition}.
 \end{aligned}$$ Moreover $$\label{eq:negative-cohomology-vanishing}
 \begin{aligned}
 H^0(V_P(j_2))&=0&&\text{in the positive decomposition},\\
 H^0(V(j_2))&=0&&\text{in the negative decomposition}.
 \end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-residual-sections"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-residual-sections</summary>

*Proof.* For the positive decomposition, apply Lemma <a href="#lem:restriction-image" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:restriction-image">31</a> to all of $H^0(P(t))$ and the triangle $P(t-1)\to P(t)\to P|_S(t)$. The increase in $h^0$ is at most the dimension of the restriction image, at most $C_2e$. Telescope over the fixed set of integers $t_0<t\leq j$, starting with <a href="#eq:initial-section-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:initial-section-vanishing">[eq:initial-section-vanishing]</a>.

For the negative decomposition, apply the lemma to the image of $H^0(E(t))$ in $H^0(P(t))$. The kernel of $$\mathbb H^0(E|_S(t))\longrightarrow\mathbb H^0(P|_S(t))$$ has dimension at most $h^1(V_0|_S(t))=O(e)$ by <a href="#eq:negative-residual-triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:negative-residual-triangle">[eq:negative-residual-triangle]</a> and <a href="#eq:negative-tail-h1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:negative-tail-h1">[eq:negative-tail-h1]</a>. Thus the restriction image of $H^0(E(t))$ itself has dimension $O(e)$. Telescope the restriction triangle for $E$ from the same vanishing at $t_0$. Finally every slope of the negative cohomology sheaves in <a href="#eq:negative-cohomology-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:negative-cohomology-vanishing">[eq:negative-cohomology-vanishing]</a>, after twisting by $j_2$, is at most $b-A+j_2<0$, by <a href="#eq:apex-A-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:apex-A-choice">[eq:apex-A-choice]</a>. They have no sections. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Fixed sheaves in positive characteristic

We have now obtained bounds involving only $e$ for the remainder and for an object of nonpositive rank. The positive tail $Q$ can have arbitrarily large rank even when $e$ is small. Its contribution will instead be bounded by a cubic expression in its narrow range of slopes.

Fix for the moment the individual object $E$. All sheaves, complexes, triangles, restriction maps and filtrations used for this $E$ can be spread to a finitely generated integral subring of $\mathbb C$. Localize so that the varieties have smooth geometrically integral projective fibers, the finitely many coherent sheaves are flat, and the displayed sequences and cohomology sheaves commute with taking fibers. Finite perfect presentations on the smooth varieties justify the same assertion for the complexes. Upper semicontinuity preserves the finitely many bounds <a href="#eq:residual-sections" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:residual-sections">[eq:residual-sections]</a> and vanishings <a href="#eq:negative-cohomology-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:negative-cohomology-vanishing">[eq:negative-cohomology-vanishing]</a> after further localization. We do not require tilt semistability on the reductions.

We will require ordinary slope semistability for finitely many fixed sheaves on $X$, on chosen surfaces, and on chosen curves. For completeness, the needed spreading assertion and its Frobenius consequence are recorded next.

<a id="stmt-lem-fixed-sheaf-spreading"></a>
<div id="lem:fixed-sheaf-spreading" class="lemma">

**Lemma 33**. *Let $G$ be a slope-semistable torsion-free sheaf on a smooth projective complex variety $Y$ with a fixed very ample class. There is a model and a localization on which the geometric fibers of $G$ are torsion-free and slope-semistable. Finitely many such requirements may be imposed simultaneously.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-fixed-sheaf-spreading"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-fixed-sheaf-spreading</summary>

*Proof.* Embed $G$ in $\mathcal O_Y(u)^k$ by choosing generators for a twist of its dual and using the injection $G\to G^{**}$. Spread this injection and make its cokernel flat, so it remains an injection on the fibers. We describe a finite-type set containing every destabilizing saturated subsheaf on every geometric fiber.

For a saturated subsheaf $J$ of rank $s$, $0<s<r(G)$, its generic Plücker coordinates extend across codimension two to sections of $\mathcal O_Y(su)\otimes(\det J)^{-1}$. If $J$ destabilizes, the degree of the zero divisor of a nonzero coordinate is bounded above by a number depending only on $u,s$ and the degree of $G$. An effective divisor of degree at most $d_0$ is contained, with its multiplicities, in the zero divisor of a section of $\mathcal O_Y(v)$ with $v\leq\max(1,d_0)$. To see this, choose a finite linear projection to projective space of dimension $\dim Y$. Each prime component has hypersurface image of degree at most its own degree. Pull back an equation for each such image and multiply with the required multiplicities. The projection is finite because its pullback of $\mathcal O(1)$ is ample.

Divide the Plücker tuple by one nonzero coordinate, then multiply by this section to clear its pole divisor. Normality extends the resulting tuple to global sections of $\mathcal O_Y(v)$. The saturated subsheaf $J$ is the kernel, inside $G$, of wedging with this tuple, viewed as a map to a fixed sum of $\mathcal O_Y(u+v)$: the kernels agree at the generic point and are saturated. The finitely many possible values of $s,v$ give finite-type spaces of tuples after shrinking the base so that their section spaces commute with base change. On each parameter space, flatten the cokernel of the universal wedge map. The kernel then commutes with taking fibers, and its rank and degree are constructible, as read from its Hilbert polynomial. Hence the condition that some tuple gives a kernel of smaller positive rank and larger slope than $G$ is constructible on the base. Allowing tuples not satisfying the Plücker equations does not cause a problem: a kernel with those numerical properties is itself a destabilizing subsheaf.

This constructible subset excludes the generic point. Otherwise a destabilizer over its algebraic closure would extend to one over $\mathbb C$. A constructible subset of an integral noetherian scheme whose closure is the whole scheme contains a nonempty open set and hence the generic point. Its closure is therefore proper; removing that closure proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-frobenius-slope-gap"></a>
<div id="lem:frobenius-slope-gap" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:frobenius-slope-gap"></a>
**Lemma 34**. *On a smooth projective geometric fiber in characteristic $p$, let $G$ be a slope-semistable torsion-free sheaf of rank $r$. For a constant $c_Y$ fixed by the chosen model of $Y$ and its polarization, $$\label{eq:frobenius-slope-gap}
 \mu_{\max}(F^*G)\leq p\mu(G)+c_Y(r-1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-frobenius-slope-gap"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-frobenius-slope-gap</summary>

*Proof.* We use the Cartier-descent argument of (Langer 2022, Theorem 4.1 and Proposition 4.2) <a id="pm-cite-74"></a>[[Langer2022](#ref-Langer2022)], keeping the chosen cotangent twist in the slope normalization. Generation of a fixed positive twist of the tangent bundle gives an embedding $\Omega_Y^1\hookrightarrow\mathcal O_Y(c_Y)^k$ with locally free cokernel. It can be chosen on the model and retained on its fibers. Frobenius is finite flat because the fiber is smooth over a perfect field.

Suppose two consecutive Harder–Narasimhan slopes of $F^*G$ differ by more than $c_Y$. For the truncation $J$ above that gap, the second fundamental map for the canonical connection is $$J\longrightarrow(F^*G/J)\otimes\Omega_Y^1.$$ It is $\mathcal O_Y$-linear. Its source has minimum slope larger than the maximum slope of its target, the latter bounded using the displayed embedding, so the map is zero. The connection thus preserves $J$. The canonical connection has zero $p$-curvature, as does its restriction to $J$. Cartier descent therefore descends this inclusion to a subsheaf of the Frobenius twist of $G$. Its slope is $\mu(J)/p>\mu(G)$, contradicting semistability. Here the Frobenius twist of the algebraically closed base field is an automorphism and does not change the slope assertion. Thus no adjacent gap is greater than $c_Y$. There are at most $r$ factors, so the maximum slope is at most the mean plus $c_Y(r-1)$, as claimed. ◻

</details>

The localizations above concern finitely many fixed sheaves, after $E$ has been chosen. The resulting base still has geometric closed fibers in unbounded prime characteristics: its constructible image in $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathbb Z$ contains the generic point and hence a nonempty open set. Very-generality was used only to choose the original complex divisors; it is not asserted for their reductions.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### The tail and the Frobenius limit

<a id="eq:twice-restricted-variance"></a>
Suppose $r(E)\geq0$ and use <a href="#eq:positive-residual-triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-residual-triangle">[eq:positive-residual-triangle]</a>. For every stable ordinary slope factor $G$ of $Q$, choose a very general smooth $S\in|H|$ and a very general $C\in|H|_S$, imposing all required exactness conditions. Refine $G|_S$ into stable factors $G_i$ and their restrictions to $C$ into stable factors $G_{ij}$. These curves, which serve only in the present algebraic argument, need not belong to the neighborhoods used for the analytic estimates. Lemma <a href="#lem:restriction-variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:restriction-variance">22</a> twice gives, with $\mu=\mu(G)$, $$\label{eq:twice-restricted-variance}
 \sum_{i,j}r_{ij}(\mu_{ij}-\mu)^2
 \leq3r(G)\delta_G^B.$$ Indeed the first variance is at most $r(G)\delta_G^B$. The sum of $r_i\delta_i^B$ on $S$ equals $r(G)\delta_G^B$ plus that variance, so is at most $2r(G)\delta_G^B$; apply the restriction estimate once more and add the two variances. The weighted means are preserved at each step. Spread this finite data and apply Lemma <a href="#lem:fixed-sheaf-spreading" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fixed-sheaf-spreading">33</a> to $G,G_i,G_{ij}$.

Since $H^2D=0$, the normalized slope shift of $L_p$ on $X,S,C$ is $-pb+O(1)$, with a bounded rounding error. Frobenius commutes with these restrictions, and its flatness preserves their exact filtrations. In the following estimates an $O_G$ constant may depend on all the fixed data chosen for $G$, but not on $p$ or on the fiber. In particular the ranks in the Frobenius slope error are now fixed, so they may enter these constants. They must not enter the coefficients which survive division by $p^3$. Lemma <a href="#lem:frobenius-slope-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:frobenius-slope-gap">34</a> gives a vanishing twist $$k_X=-p(\mu-b)+O_G(1),\qquad
 H^0(F^*G\otimes L_p(k_X))=0,$$ where the bounded integer adjustment is chosen to make the maximum slope negative. Telescope from $k_X$ to zero using the surface restriction sequence. At each integer $k$ in this interval, bound the surface sections by those of its factors $G_i$. For each such factor telescope on $S$ from its own vanishing twist $-p(\mu_i-b)+O_G(1)$ up to $k$, if $k$ exceeds that twist.

<a id="eq:curve-ramp-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:surface-square-bound"></a>
On $C$, the $H$-degree is $h$. Taking successive jets at a point until the maximum ordinary degree slope is negative, and using <a href="#eq:frobenius-slope-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frobenius-slope-gap">[eq:frobenius-slope-gap]</a>, gives for every factor $$\label{eq:curve-ramp-bound}
 h^0(F^*G_{ij}\otimes L_p(l))
 \leq h r_{ij}\bigl(p(\mu_{ij}-b)+l\bigr)_++O_G(1).$$ The error includes the integer rounding and at most a bounded number of point jets, each of dimension $r_{ij}$; all ranks here are fixed before $p$ varies. Each surface telescope has only $O_G(p)$ terms in the range under consideration. Summing the ramp in <a href="#eq:curve-ramp-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:curve-ramp-bound">[eq:curve-ramp-bound]</a>, and enlarging the sum down to all its positive terms when necessary, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 h^0(F^*G|_S\otimes L_p(k))
 &\leq\frac{hp^2}{2}\sum_{i,j}r_{ij}
        (\mu_{ij}-b+k/p)_+^2+O_G(p)\notag\\
 &\leq\frac{hp^2}{2}\bigl[
 r(G)(\mu-b+k/p)_+^2+3r(G)\delta_G^B\bigr]+O_G(p).
 \label{eq:surface-square-bound}
\end{aligned}$$ For the second inequality use $x_+^2\leq y_+^2+2y_+(x-y)+(x-y)^2$ and the vanishing of the weighted first displacement from the mean, followed by <a href="#eq:twice-restricted-variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:twice-restricted-variance">[eq:twice-restricted-variance]</a>. The ramp sum differs from the corresponding half-square by $O_G(p)$, uniformly for the indices used above.

<a id="eq:tail-frobenius"></a>
The outer telescope has length $p(\mu-b)+O_G(1)\leq(A+1)p+O_G(1)$. Summing <a href="#eq:surface-square-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:surface-square-bound">[eq:surface-square-bound]</a> and using the Riemann sum for the square on $[-(\mu-b),0]$ gives the following bound. Here and below, limits are taken along geometric fibers of the chosen model, after all the specified localizations, with characteristics $p$ tending to infinity: <a id="eq:tail-factor-frobenius"></a>$$\label{eq:tail-factor-frobenius}
 \limsup_{p\to\infty}p^{-3}h^0(F^*G\otimes L_p)
 \leq\frac{h r(G)}6(\mu-b)^3
       +C_Ah r(G)\delta_G^B.$$ The summed $O_G(p)$ errors are $O_G(p^2)$ and vanish in this limit. One may take a fixed multiple of $A+1$ for $C_A$; it does not depend on the restriction sheaves or their ranks. Summing over the finite filtration of $Q$, using <a href="#eq:positive-tail-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-tail-moments">[eq:positive-tail-moments]</a>, gives $$\label{eq:tail-frobenius}
 \limsup_{p\to\infty}p^{-3}h^0(F^*Q\otimes L_p)
 \leq\frac{hA^2}{6}d_B(Q)+O(e).$$ To verify its leading term, write $\mu-b=A+s$, $0\leq s\leq1$. Then $(A+s)^3-A^2(A+s)\leq C_As$, and $\sum r_is_i\leq e$ by <a href="#eq:positive-excess" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-excess">[eq:positive-excess]</a>.

<a id="eq:residual-frobenius"></a>
It remains to bound the residual contribution and the positive even cohomology group in degree two. Let $P_1$ be the positive residual $P$ or a nonpositive-rank object treated by the negative decomposition. It has ordinary cohomology in degrees $[-1,0]$, and <a href="#eq:negative-cohomology-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:negative-cohomology-vanishing">[eq:negative-cohomology-vanishing]</a> supplies the vanishing required in Corollary <a href="#cor:frobenius-section-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:frobenius-section-transfer">30</a>. That corollary and <a href="#eq:residual-sections" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:residual-sections">[eq:residual-sections]</a> give, for either rounding line $L$, $$\label{eq:residual-frobenius}
 h^0(F^*P_1\otimes L)
 \leq N_{p,L}h^0(P_1(j))\leq C_4p^3e.$$ The characteristic-zero section bounds pass to these fibers by the finite semicontinuity conditions already imposed.

<a id="eq:apex-duality-triangle"></a>
<a id="eq:degree-two-frobenius"></a>
Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:tilt-duality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tilt-duality">21</a> to $E$: $$\label{eq:apex-duality-triangle}
 \widetilde E\longrightarrow R\mathcal Hom(E,\mathcal O_X)[1]
 \longrightarrow T_0[-1],$$ where $T_0$ is zero-dimensional and $\widetilde E$ is finite-slope semistable of slope zero at $(A,-B)$. Its rank is $-r(E)$, its denominator is $d(E)$, its excess is $e$, and $z_{-B}(\widetilde E)=z_B(E)+\operatorname{length}(T_0)/h$. Include this triangle and the negative decomposition for $\widetilde E$ in the fixed spreading data. Serre duality, flatness of $F$, and <a href="#eq:apex-duality-triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:apex-duality-triangle">[eq:apex-duality-triangle]</a> give $$\label{eq:degree-two-frobenius}
 h^2(F^*E\otimes L_p)
 =h^0(F^*\widetilde E\otimes L'_p)\leq C_4p^3e.$$ For clarity, Serre duality first identifies the dual of the degree-two group with degree zero of $F^*R\mathcal Hom(E,\mathcal O_X)[1]\otimes L'_p$. The term $F^*T_0[-1]\otimes L'_p$ has zero hypercohomology in degrees $-1$ and $0$, so it does not alter that group.

<a id="eq:euler-frobenius-bound"></a>
The complex $F^*E\otimes L_p$ has hypercohomology in degrees $[-1,3]$. The only even degrees in this interval are zero and two, whence $$\chi(F^*E\otimes L_p)
 \leq h^0(F^*E\otimes L_p)+h^2(F^*E\otimes L_p).$$ The degree-zero group is bounded above by the sum of the corresponding groups for $P$ and $Q$ in <a href="#eq:positive-residual-triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-residual-triangle">[eq:positive-residual-triangle]</a>. Equations <a href="#eq:tail-frobenius" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-frobenius">[eq:tail-frobenius]</a>, <a href="#eq:residual-frobenius" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:residual-frobenius">[eq:residual-frobenius]</a> and <a href="#eq:degree-two-frobenius" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:degree-two-frobenius">[eq:degree-two-frobenius]</a> therefore imply $$\label{eq:euler-frobenius-bound}
 \limsup_{p\to\infty}p^{-3}\chi(F^*E\otimes L_p)
 \leq\frac{hA^2}{6}d_B(Q)+C_5e.$$

<a id="eq:rr-frobenius-limit"></a>
Riemann–Roch identifies the limit on the left: $$\label{eq:rr-frobenius-limit}
 \lim_{p\to\infty}p^{-3}\chi(F^*E\otimes L_p)=h z_B(E).$$ Here is a way to compare varying characteristics without identifying their cohomology rings. On a smooth fiber, $\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_i(F^*E)=p^i\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_i(E)$ by the splitting principle. The same formula holds for the Adams class $\psi^p[E]$ in vector-bundle $K$-theory. Riemann–Roch thus identifies the Euler characteristic in <a href="#eq:rr-frobenius-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rr-frobenius-limit">[eq:rr-frobenius-limit]</a> with that of $\psi^p[E]\otimes L_p$. This class is defined by exterior-power operations on a fixed perfect presentation and commutes with specialization. Its Euler characteristic is constant from the model to the complex fiber. On that fiber, write $c_1(L_p)=-pB+R_p$, where $R_p$ remains in a fixed bounded set of divisor classes. The degree-three leading term of $$\int_X\left(\sum_{i=0}^3p^i\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_i(E)\right)
                  e^{-pB+R_p}\mathop{\mathrm{td}}(X)$$ is $p^3\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_3^B(E)$; all other terms are $O_E(p^2)$. This proves <a href="#eq:rr-frobenius-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rr-frobenius-limit">[eq:rr-frobenius-limit]</a>, without a condition on $K_X$.

Combining <a href="#eq:euler-frobenius-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:euler-frobenius-bound">[eq:euler-frobenius-bound]</a> and <a href="#eq:rr-frobenius-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rr-frobenius-limit">[eq:rr-frobenius-limit]</a>, and using $d_B(Q)\leq d_B(E)$, proves <a href="#eq:apex-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:apex-estimate">[eq:apex-estimate]</a> for $r(E)\geq0$. The surviving constants came only from the fixed regularity, surface estimates and the interval $[A,A+1]$; every constant depending on the individual spread sheaves occurred in an error of order at most $p^2$ and disappeared after division by $p^3$. This establishes the asserted order of quantifiers.

If $r(E)<0$, apply the just-proved case to $\widetilde E$ in <a href="#eq:apex-duality-triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:apex-duality-triangle">[eq:apex-duality-triangle]</a>, which has positive rank. Its $d$ and $e$ agree with those of $E$, and its third character is at least $z_B(E)$. The estimate for $\widetilde E$ therefore implies the estimate for $E$. Our constants were chosen for both signs of $B_0$, so this use of the mirror parameters costs nothing. Finally undo the integral tensor normalization of $b$. This completes the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:apex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:apex">28</a>.

<a id="sec:wall"></a>
### From one apex to uniform inequalities

The fixed-apex estimate now supplies the global inequality. The mechanism is numerical: a differential inequality preserves a strict violation as the volume parameter moves toward the apex. If an object becomes strictly semistable, one continues with a violating stable factor. A discrete discriminant makes this process finite. Transport along a zero-slope hyperbola and induction on the discriminant appear in (Bayer, Macrì, and Stellari 2016, sec. 5) <a id="pm-cite-75"></a>[[BMS2016](#ref-BMS2016)] and, for a corrected inequality, (Bernardara et al. 2017, Lemma 2.7) <a id="pm-cite-76"></a>[[BMSZ2017](#ref-BMSZ2017)]. Here the terminal estimate is at a fixed positive volume $A$. The comparison functions below turn that estimate into an all-volume correction and an uncorrected tail.

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### A transport principle

At slope zero define the defect and the nonnegative comparison quantity <a id="eq:wall-defect"></a>$$\label{eq:wall-defect}
 D_a(E)=z_b(E)-\frac{a^2d_b(E)}6,\qquad
 S_a(E)=\frac{\Delta(E)}{d_b(E)}
       =d_b(E)-\frac{a^2r(E)^2}{d_b(E)}.$$ They are defined whenever $d_b(E)>0$ and $w_b(E)=a^2r(E)/2$. In particular $0\le S_a\le d_b$ for a semistable object.

<a id="stmt-prop-apex-transport"></a>
<div id="prop:apex-transport" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:wall-apex-input"></a>
<a id="eq:wall-constants"></a>
**Proposition 35** (Transport from a fixed apex). *Fix a smooth projective complex threefold $X$, an integral ample class $H$, and $B_0\in N^1(X)_{\mathbb Q}$. Suppose that there are $A>0$ and $C\ge0$ such that every finite-slope tilt-semistable object of slope zero at $(A,b)$, for every $b\in\mathbb R$, satisfies $$\label{eq:wall-apex-input}
 D_A(E)\le C S_A(E).$$ Then, with $$\label{eq:wall-constants}
 R_0=A+\frac{3C}{A},\qquad
 k_0=\max\left\{C+\frac{A^2}{6},\frac{CR_0}{A}\right\},$$ every finite-slope tilt-semistable object of slope zero at $(a,b)$ satisfies $D_a(E)\le k_0d_b(E)$ for all $a>0,b\in\mathbb R$, and $D_a(E)\le0$ whenever $a>R_0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-apex-transport"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-apex-transport</summary>

<a id="eq:zero-branch"></a>
<a id="eq:wall-comparison-derivative"></a>
*Proof.* We first compute along the slope-zero curve of a fixed class. Its positive-denominator branch is $$\label{eq:zero-branch}
 d(a)=\sqrt{\Delta+a^2r^2},\qquad
 b(a)=\frac{d_{B_0}-d(a)}r\quad(r\ne0).$$ For rank zero, $d$ and $b$ remain constant. If the branch starts at a semistable object, then $\Delta\ge0$, and its denominator stays positive at every positive $a$. Indeed $d(a)\ge a|r|$ when $r\ne0$, whereas $d$ is a positive constant when $r=0$. The identities $dz_b/db=-w_b$ and $w_b=a^2r/2$ give <a id="eq:wall-derivatives"></a>$$\label{eq:wall-derivatives}
 D_a'=-\frac a3S_a,\qquad
 S_a'=-\frac{ar^2}{d(a)^2}S_a.$$ For example, when $r\ne0$ one has $d'=ar^2/d$, $b'=-ar/d$, and $z'=a^3r^2/(2d)$; these give both equalities directly. The rank-zero case follows from the definitions. For a differentiable nonnegative function $k$, therefore, $$\label{eq:wall-comparison-derivative}
 (D_a-k(a)S_a)'=
 S_a\left(-\frac a3-k'(a)+k(a)\frac{ar^2}{d(a)^2}\right).$$

There are three comparisons. For the corrected bound below $A$, take $k(a)=C+(A^2-a^2)/6$ and move upward toward $A$. The right side of <a href="#eq:wall-comparison-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wall-comparison-derivative">[eq:wall-comparison-derivative]</a> is $k(a)ar^2S_a/d(a)^2\ge0$, so a strict violation persists upward. To rule out positive defect at a starting value $R>R_0$, take $k(a)=a(R-a)/3$ on $[A,R]$ and move downward. Since $ar^2/d(a)^2\le1/a$, $$-\frac a3-k'(a)+k(a)\frac{ar^2}{d(a)^2}
 \le-\frac a3-k'(a)+\frac{k(a)}a=0.$$ A strict violation again persists in the direction of movement. Here $k(R)=0$ and $k(A)=A(R-A)/3>C$. For the corrected bound in the remaining interval, on any interval $[A,R]$ use $k(a)=Ca/A$ and move downward. The same upper bound for the last term gives a derivative at most $-aS_a/3\le0$, and $k(A)=C$.

Each comparison preserves a strict violation while the class remains stable. We next show how to continue through a change of stability, with a strict decrease of a discrete discriminant at each replacement. For an equal-slope-zero filtration with stable factors $E_i$, all $d_i$ are positive and $w_i=a^2r_i/2$. Additivity and weighted Cauchy–Schwarz give <a id="eq:wall-additivity"></a>$$\label{eq:wall-additivity}
 D_a(E)=\sum_iD_a(E_i),\qquad
 S_a(E)=\sum_i d_i-a^2\frac{(\sum_i r_i)^2}{\sum_i d_i}
        \ge\sum_iS_a(E_i).$$ Thus a strict inequality $D_a(E)>k(a)S_a(E)$, with $k(a)\ge0$, passes to at least one stable factor. Such a factorization is finite by Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-tilt-factors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-tilt-factors">19</a>.

Start with a violating stable factor and follow its branch <a href="#eq:zero-branch" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:zero-branch">[eq:zero-branch]</a> toward $A$ as long as it remains stable. Stability is open. At an endpoint in the positive $a$-interval, continuity of extremal phases gives semistability, since the formal denominator remains positive. The strict violation persists at the endpoint: along the movement $D-kS$ is at least its initial positive value. If the endpoint is $A$, this contradicts <a href="#eq:wall-apex-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wall-apex-input">[eq:wall-apex-input]</a>. Otherwise the endpoint is nonstable, since stability there would extend the interval, and we split again and select a violating stable factor.

<a id="eq:wall-discriminant-split"></a>
At every such genuine wall, the discriminant of each factor is strictly smaller than that of its parent. Indeed, $$\label{eq:wall-discriminant-split}
 \Delta(E)=\sum_i\Delta(E_i)+
    2\sum_{i<j}(d_i d_j-a^2r_i r_j).$$ Every term is nonnegative because $d_i\ge a|r_i|$. If all cross terms vanished, all $r_i$ would be nonzero with the same sign and $d_i=a|r_i|$; hence all factors would have $\Delta(E_i)=0$ and proportional triples $(r_i,d_i,w_i)$. Twisting preserves this proportionality. Openness keeps the finitely many stable factors stable near the wall, so their fixed extension triangles make $E$ an extension of objects with equal finite slope immediately before the wall as well. The filtration has at least two factors, so this contradicts the preceding stability of $E$. Some cross term in <a href="#eq:wall-discriminant-split" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wall-discriminant-split">[eq:wall-discriminant-split]</a> is therefore positive, and every factor has smaller discriminant.

Because $B_0$ is rational, formula <a href="#eq:projected-discriminant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projected-discriminant">[eq:projected-discriminant]</a> puts all these discriminants in $N^{-1}\mathbb Z$ for one positive integer $N$ depending only on $X,H,B_0$. They are nonnegative on the semistable factors. Strict descent consequently permits only finitely many wall splits. This also excludes an accumulating sequence of walls with changing factors: every change consumes at least $1/N$ of discriminant, and every final stable interval either reaches $A$ or has an interior semistable endpoint at which another such split is necessary. There is no denominator-zero endpoint inside the interval, by <a href="#eq:zero-branch" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:zero-branch">[eq:zero-branch]</a>. The proposed violation must therefore reach $A$, giving a contradiction in each of the three comparisons.

The first comparison proves $D_a\le(C+(A^2-a^2)/6)S_a$ for $0<a\le A$. The second proves $D_a\le0$ for $a>R_0$. The third proves $D_a\le(Ca/A)S_a$ for $A\le a\le R_0$. Finally use $S_a\le d_b$ and <a href="#eq:wall-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wall-constants">[eq:wall-constants]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### The corrected inequality and the uncorrected tail

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-apex-transport-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-apex-transport</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:uniform-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:uniform-inequality">2</a>.* First use the very-ample and transverse-twist reductions of Section <a href="#sec:tilt" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tilt">4</a>. Proposition <a href="#prop:apex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:apex">28</a> supplies $A>0$ and $C\ge0$, chosen independently of $E$ and $b$, such that $$D_A(E)\le C\bigl(d_B(E)-A|r(E)|\bigr).$$ At slope zero put $e=d_B-A|r|$. Since $d_B\ge A|r|$, $$S_A=\frac{(d_B-A|r|)(d_B+A|r|)}{d_B},\qquad
 e\le S_A\le2e.$$ The hypothesis of Proposition <a href="#prop:apex-transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:apex-transport">35</a> follows. Multiplying its conclusion by $h$ gives $$\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_3^B(E)\le\frac{a^2}{6}H^2\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_1^B(E)
                      +k_0H^2\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_1^B(E),$$ and gives the same inequality without the correction for $a>R_0$. Undo the polarization rescaling as described in Section <a href="#sec:tilt" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tilt">4</a>. This gives constants $k\ge0$ and $R_*>0$ for the original $X,H,B_0$, uniform in both $E$ and $b$.

Enlarge $k$ to a rational nonnegative number. The curve class $\Gamma=kH^2\in N_1(X)_{\mathbb Q}$ then satisfies $\Gamma\cdot H=kh\ge0$, and the correction is exactly $\Gamma\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_1^B(E)$. This proves the corrected statement with its required rational class, as well as the independent uncorrected large-volume threshold. No canonical-bundle hypothesis has entered either argument. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### The quadratic inequality at every slope

<a id="eq:alternative-slope"></a>
<a id="eq:quadratic-form"></a>
We finish by expressing the uncorrected tail in the alternative coordinates used for generalized Bogomolov–Gieseker inequalities. Here $B_0=0$ and $$v(E)=(v_0,v_1,v_2,v_3)
 =(H^3\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_0(E),H^2\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_1(E),H\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_2(E),\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_3(E)).$$ For $\alpha>\beta^2/2$, the first tilt is $\mathcal B_{H,\beta H}$ and its slope is $$\label{eq:alternative-slope}
 \widehat\nu_{\alpha,\beta}(E)=
 \frac{v_2(E)-\beta v_1(E)+(\beta^2-\alpha)v_0(E)}
      {v_1(E)-\beta v_0(E)},$$ with value $+\infty$ at zero denominator. Define $$\label{eq:quadratic-form}
 Q_{\alpha,\beta}(v)=
 \alpha(v_1^2-2v_0v_2)+\beta(3v_0v_3-v_1v_2)
                      +2v_2^2-3v_1v_3.$$ Thus no correction of any character is being incorporated into $v$.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-apex-transport-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-apex-transport</summary>

<a id="eq:quadratic-twisted"></a>
*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:quadratic-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:quadratic-tail">3</a>.* Take the threshold of Theorem <a href="#thm:uniform-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:uniform-inequality">2</a> with $B_0=0$. Set $b=\beta$ and $a=\sqrt{2\alpha-b^2}$. The numerator in <a href="#eq:alternative-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:alternative-slope">[eq:alternative-slope]</a> is $h(w_b-a^2r/2)$ and its denominator is $hd_b$, so its slope is exactly $\nu_{a,b}$. Expanding <a href="#eq:quadratic-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadratic-form">[eq:quadratic-form]</a> with <a href="#eq:twist-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:twist-two">[eq:twist-two]</a>–<a href="#eq:twist-three" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:twist-three">[eq:twist-three]</a> gives $$\label{eq:quadratic-twisted}
 \frac{Q_{\alpha,b}(v(E))}{h^2}
    =\frac{a^2\Delta(E)}2+2w_b(E)^2-3d_b(E)z_b(E).$$ If $d_b(E)=0$, this is nonnegative by <a href="#eq:tilt-discriminants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tilt-discriminants">[eq:tilt-discriminants]</a>; it includes zero-dimensional objects.

Otherwise put $u=\nu_{a,b}(E)$, $c=b+u$, and $R=\sqrt{a^2+u^2}$. Lemma <a href="#lem:tilt-semicircle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tilt-semicircle">20</a> makes $E$ semistable of slope zero at $(R,c)$, with $d_c>0$. Using $w_b=ud_b+a^2r/2$, $$d_c=d_b-ur,\qquad
 z_c=z_b-uw_b+\tfrac12u^2d_b-\tfrac16u^3r$$ in <a href="#eq:quadratic-twisted" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadratic-twisted">[eq:quadratic-twisted]</a> gives the exact identity <a id="eq:quadratic-zero-slope-identity"></a>$$\label{eq:quadratic-zero-slope-identity}
 \frac{Q_{\alpha,b}(v(E))}{h^2}
       =-3d_b(E)\left(z_c(E)-\frac{R^2d_c(E)}6\right).$$ Since $\alpha>f(b)$ implies $R\ge a>R_*$, the expression in parentheses is nonpositive by the uncorrected tail. This proves the claim. The passage uses the entire semistability arc and the inequality $R\ge a$; an all-parameter equivalence alone would not justify a conclusion on this truncated region. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:counterexamples"></a>
### Comparison with the known counterexamples

The uncorrected strong inequality fails at small volume, even on Calabi–Yau threefolds. We record the parameters of two established counterexamples and then give a uniform estimate for sheaves on their exceptional surfaces. Throughout this section, the volume parameter is $a$ in the ordinary tilt slope $\nu_{a,b}$; the physical volume is $t=\sqrt3a$.

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### A line bundle and a contracted divisor

<a id="eq:schmidt-disk"></a>
Let $X=\operatorname{Bl}_p\mathbb P^3$, let $L$ be the pulled-back hyperplane class, and let $E$ be the exceptional divisor. Fix $H=2L-E$ and $B_0=0$. The intersection identities $L^3=E^3=1$, $LE=0$, and $H^3=7$ give $$\bigl(H^3\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_0,H^2\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_1,H\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_2,\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_3\bigr)(\mathcal O_X(L))
 =\left(7,4,1,\frac16\right).$$ For the conventional quadratic expression $$\mathcal Q_{a,b}(F)
 =a^2\bigl((H^2\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_1(F))^2-2H^3\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_0(F)H\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_2(F)\bigr)
  +4(H\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_2^{bH}(F))^2
  -6H^2\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_1^{bH}(F)\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_3^{bH}(F),$$ direct expansion yields $$\mathcal Q_{a,b}(\mathcal O_X(L))
 =2\left(a^2+\left(b-\frac14\right)^2-\frac1{16}\right).
 \label{eq:schmidt-disk}$$ Schmidt proves that actual tilt-stable points occur in the negative region of <a href="#eq:schmidt-disk" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:schmidt-disk">[eq:schmidt-disk]</a> (Schmidt 2017, Theorem 3.1). All these violations have $a<1/4$. On the positive-denominator zero-slope branch one has, more explicitly, $$a^2=b^2-\frac87b+\frac27,
 \qquad b<\frac{4-\sqrt2}{7},
 \qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_3^{bH}(\mathcal O_X(L))
 -\frac{a^2}{6}H^2\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_1^{bH}(\mathcal O_X(L))
 =\frac{4b-1}{42}.$$ These formulas use the same ordinary characters and normalization as the strong inequality.

<a id="eq:divisor-counterexample"></a>
For an integral effective divisor $D\subset X$, still with $B_0=0$, the identity $\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}(\mathcal O_D)=1-e^{-D}$ shows that its unique zero-slope line and its defect there are $$\begin{aligned}
 b_0&=-\frac{HD^2}{2H^2D},\notag\\
 \mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_3^{b_0H}(\mathcal O_D)
 -\frac{a^2}{6}H^2D
 &=\frac{D^3}{6}
   -\frac{(HD^2)^2}{8H^2D}
   -\frac{a^2}{6}H^2D.
 \label{eq:divisor-counterexample}
\end{aligned}$$ The wall-radius argument in (Martinez and Schmidt 2019, Lemma 3.1 and its proof) <a id="pm-cite-77"></a>[[MartinezSchmidt2019](#ref-MartinezSchmidt2019)] gives semistability on this line for $a\ge H^2D/(2H^3)$, and stability for the strict inequality in our subobject-versus-quotient convention. Indeed, every positive-rank wall has radius at most this bound, while a proper rank-zero subobject of $\mathcal O_D$ is a subsheaf with quotient supported in dimension at most one, hence of infinite tilt slope.

<a id="eq:contracted-defect"></a>
Suppose now that $D$ contracts to a point, $-D$ is relatively ample, and $H=ML-D$, where $L$ is the pullback of an ample divisor and $M$ is sufficiently large. Put $s=D^3>0$. Since $LD=0$, one has $H^2D=s$ and $HD^2=-s$. Formula <a href="#eq:divisor-counterexample" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-counterexample">[eq:divisor-counterexample]</a> becomes $$b_0=\frac12,
 \qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_3^{b_0H}(\mathcal O_D)-\frac{a^2}{6}H^2D
 =s\left(\frac1{24}-\frac{a^2}{6}\right).
 \label{eq:contracted-defect}$$ Thus, when $H^3>s$, the interval $s/(2H^3)<a<1/2$ consists of stable counterexamples. The polarization, including $M$, is fixed in this assertion. Tensoring by $\mathcal O_X(kH)$ translates $b$ by $k$ and preserves the twisted characters and $a$; it gives counterexamples with unbounded $b$, but with the same bounded volume interval.

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### A uniform estimate on the reduced exceptional surface

The same upper bound on the violating volume applies to sheaves of arbitrary rank on a smooth reduced exceptional surface, whenever their pushforwards are tilt-semistable. The following statement also allows twists transverse to the polarization.

<a id="stmt-lem-exceptional-surface-bound"></a>
<div id="lem:exceptional-surface-bound" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:exceptional-uniform"></a>
**Lemma 36**. *Let $X$ be a smooth projective complex threefold, let $H$ be an ample integral divisor, and let $i:D\hookrightarrow X$ be a smooth integral divisor such that $H|_D=-D|_D$. Fix $B_0\in N^1(X)_{\mathbb Q}$, and set $B=B_0+bH$. If $F$ is a torsion-free sheaf of positive rank on $D$ and $i_*F$ is $\nu_{a,b}$-semistable of slope zero, then $$\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_3^B(i_*F)}{H^2\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_1^B(i_*F)}\le\frac1{24}.
 \label{eq:exceptional-uniform}$$ Consequently these objects satisfy the corrected strong inequality with $\Gamma=H^2/24$ for every $a>0$, and the uncorrected strong inequality for every $a\ge1/2$. Both constants are independent of $F$, its rank, and $b$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-exceptional-surface-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-exceptional-surface-bound</summary>

*Proof.* Write $\ell=H|_D$, $s=\ell^2=D^3>0$, $\rho=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}(F)$, and $C=B_0|_D$. Put $$u=\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_1^C(F),\qquad v=\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_2^C(F),\qquad
 \mu=\frac{\ell u}{\rho s},\qquad
 \delta=\mu^2-\frac{2v}{\rho s}.$$ Every exact sequence of sheaves on $D$ pushes forward to an exact sequence of torsion sheaves in the first-tilt heart. Their tilt slopes differ from their $C$-twisted normalized surface slopes by the common constant $1/2-b$. Twisting shifts all surface slopes equally, so tilt semistability of $i_*F$ implies slope semistability of $F$ for $\ell$. The classical surface Bogomolov–Gieseker inequality (Bayer, Macrì, and Toda 2014, Theorem 3.1.4) <a id="pm-cite-78"></a>[[BMT2014](#ref-BMT2014)] and the Hodge index theorem give $$u^2-2\rho v\ge0,
 \qquad (\ell u)^2\ge s u^2,
 \qquad \delta\ge0.$$ The first expression is unchanged by twisting the Chern character by $C$, so these conclusions hold for the specified $B_0$.

The normal line bundle has first Chern class $-\ell$, and hence $\mathop{\mathrm{td}}(N_{D/X})^{-1}=1+\ell/2+\ell^2/6$. Grothendieck–Riemann–Roch now gives $$H^2\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_1^B(i_*F)=\rho s,
 \qquad
 H\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_2^B(i_*F)=\rho s(\mu+1/2-b).$$ Since the threefold rank of $i_*F$ is zero, its zero-slope condition is $b=\mu+1/2$. The degree-three term of the same formula yields $$\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}_3^B(i_*F)}{\rho s}
 =\frac{v}{\rho s}+\frac\mu2+\frac16
   -b(\mu+1/2)+\frac{b^2}{2}
 =\frac1{24}-\frac\delta2\le\frac1{24}.$$ This proves <a href="#eq:exceptional-uniform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exceptional-uniform">[eq:exceptional-uniform]</a>; the two strong bounds follow by subtracting $a^2/6$. ◻

</details>

For the Weierstrass examples of (Martinez and Schmidt 2019, sec. 3.2 and Appendix A) <a id="pm-cite-79"></a>[[MartinezSchmidt2019](#ref-MartinezSchmidt2019)], let $p:X\to S$ be a Weierstrass elliptic fibration from a smooth Calabi–Yau threefold to a del Pezzo surface, and let $\Sigma$ be its section. Since $K_X\simeq\mathcal O_X$, adjunction gives $\Sigma|_\Sigma=K_S$. For the ample polarization $H=q\Sigma-(1+q)p^*K_S$, with a positive integer $q$, one has $H|_\Sigma=-K_S$. Thus Lemma <a href="#lem:exceptional-surface-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:exceptional-surface-bound">36</a> applies in arbitrary rank and after every line-bundle twist on $\Sigma$ whose pushforward is tilt-semistable. In particular, the known Calabi–Yau counterexample has the defect <a href="#eq:contracted-defect" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contracted-defect">[eq:contracted-defect]</a>, with $s=K_S^2$, and its violating volume range is bounded by $a<1/2$.

<a id="stmt-rem-nonreduced-multiple"></a>
<div id="rem:nonreduced-multiple" class="remark">

*Remark 37*. Replacing $D$ by $nD$ in the character calculation does not produce a stable counterexample at arbitrarily large volume. Under the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:exceptional-surface-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:exceptional-surface-bound">36</a>, the exact sequence $$0\longrightarrow\mathcal O_D(-(n-1)D)\longrightarrow\mathcal O_{nD}
 \longrightarrow\mathcal O_{(n-1)D}\longrightarrow0$$ lies in every first-tilt heart. For $B_0=0$ and $b=n/2$, its three slopes are $(n-1)/2$, $0$, and $-1/2$, respectively. Thus $\mathcal O_{nD}$ is unstable for every $a>0$ when $n>1$. The uniform estimate above concerns sheaves on the reduced divisor; its proof uses surface semistability, rather than the character of an arbitrary multiple.

</div>

          "source_object": "fig:0001"

      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n box/.style={draw,rounded corners=2pt,align=center,font=\\small,\n             text width=4.4cm,minimum height=10mm,inner sep=5pt},\n arrow/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]},thick}]\n\\node[box] (ambient) {Weighted ambient stability\\\\and support};\n\\node[box,below=7mm of ambient] (deform) {Exact charge deformation\\\\and restriction to $X$};\n\\node[box,below=7mm of deform] (support) {Full numerical support\\\\and stable points for both charges};\n\\node[box,below=7mm of support] (heart) {Ordinary charge: surface comparison\\\\and the double-tilt heart};\n\\node[box,right=15mm of ambient] (analysis) {Rank-uniform restriction\\\\and cohomology};\n\\node[above=3mm of ambient,align=center,font=\\small] {Prescribed charges: $K_X\\simeq\\OO_X$};\n\\node[above=3mm of analysis,align=center,font=\\small] {Inequalities: arbitrary threefold};\n\\node[box,below=7mm of analysis] (apex) {Fixed-volume defect bound};\n\\node[box,below=7mm of apex] (wall) {Wall transport};\n\\node[box,below=7mm of wall] (bounds) {Corrected all-volume inequality\\\\and uncorrected tail};\n\\draw[arrow] (ambient)--(deform);\n\\draw[arrow] (deform)--(support);\n\\draw[arrow] (support)--(heart);\n\\draw[arrow] (analysis)--(apex);\n\\draw[arrow] (apex)--(wall);\n\\draw[arrow] (wall)--(bounds);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Flowchart diagram with two parallel columns of rectangular boxes and downward arrows.\nLeft column, headed \"Prescribed charges: $K_X \\simeq \\mathcal{O}_X$\":\n- Box 1: \"Weighted ambient stability and support\"\n  $\\downarrow$\n- Box 2: \"Exact charge deformation and restriction to $X$\"\n  $\\downarrow$\n- Box 3: \"Full numerical support and stable points for both charges\"\n  $\\downarrow$\n- Box 4: \"Ordinary charge: surface comparison and the double-tilt heart\"\n\nRight column, headed \"Inequalities: arbitrary threefold\":\n- Box 1: \"Rank-uniform restriction and cohomology\"\n  $\\downarrow$\n- Box 2: \"Fixed-volume defect bound\"\n  $\\downarrow$\n- Box 3: \"Wall transport\"\n  $\\downarrow$\n- Box 4: \"Corrected all-volume inequality and uncorrected tail\""
